Tool reply2026-09-23T04:28:41.645Z
1	# Inference Simulator — 01 Product and Rationale
2	
3	Status: scoping complete (September 2026). Personal project; build not started.
4	Related docs: 02-simulator, 03-benchmarks, 04-stack, 05-ui-ux, 06-deployment.
5	
6	## 1. Product statement
7	
8	An interactive browser simulator of what happens when you serve a large language model inside a fixed-capacity enclave: a known number of GPUs, a known analyst population, no autoscaling, and no way to add hardware when load spikes.
9	
10	The user scales from one analyst on one GPU, to many analysts on one GPU, to many analysts across several replicas. Throughput, time-to-first-token (TTFT), tail latency, and KV cache utilization respond live. Six one-click behaviours each isolate one lesson. A telemetry toggle shows the same run as the off-site team would see it: a delayed, aggregated daily rollup.
11	
12	## 2. Audience
13	
14	Forward-deployed engineers, netops, sysops, product managers, and others who deploy and maintain LLM systems on FedRAMP and IC high-side networks (IL5, IL6, top secret networks such as C2E). These systems have real scaling constraints and little visibility into system behavior and logging. The audience is highly technical but often unfamiliar with inference internals (prefill vs. decode, batching, KV cache). The goal is intuition about the systems they maintain.
15	
16	| Persona | Brings | Takes away |
17	|---|---|---|
18	| FDE | Deploys and configures serving stacks | Routing policy choice; session affinity; how engine behavior drives latency |
19	| NetOps | Load balancers, sticky sessions, consistent hashing | Breaking stickiness costs GPU time; remapping after failure; retry amplification |
20	| SysOps | Owns hosts, outages, recovery | Replica loss and rejoin; cold start; preemption; what rollups hide |
21	| PM | Owns capacity and budget | N replicas do not deliver N× capacity; the memory ceiling; what "healthy" averages hide |
22	
23	Design rules:
24	
25	- **Single stream.** One teaching path for everyone; no role-based paths (they would multiply the complexity tree).
26	- **Personas as a review checklist.** Check every feature against all four personas during the build.
27	- **Tooltips orient; scenarios teach.**
28	
29	## 3. Rationale
30	
31	1. **Fixed capacity.** The enclave has a fixed number of GPUs and a known analyst population. There is no autoscaling and no way to add hardware during a spike. Every lesson happens inside that constraint.
32	2. **Progressive complexity.** One analyst on one GPU, then many analysts on one GPU, then many analysts across replicas. Each stage adds one dimension and ends at the limit that motivates the next.
33	3. **The ceiling is memory, not compute.** Engineers arriving from web services assume they run out of CPU. Here nvidia-smi reports the GPU as busy, compute utilization shows headroom, and KV cache sits at 98%. The scheduler begins preempting running requests and recomputing prefill it already paid for. Throughput looks flat and healthy while p99 knees upward, which average latency hides completely.
34	4. **Least-busy routing can be the wrong answer.** Round-robin ignores load. Least-outstanding counts requests, not their cost, because output length is unknown at dispatch. It also ignores where a conversation's history is cached: an analyst's follow-up sent to the idle replica re-prefills the whole conversation. Session-aware routing trades some load imbalance for a large TTFT win on returning turns.
35	5. **You cannot see the system you are operating.** On the high side there is no live dashboard, no phone-home telemetry, and no engineer connecting from outside. What leaves the enclave is a reviewed, aggregated rollup arriving hours or days later. The simulator shows the same incident twice: the god view, then the egress view, where the spike flattens into a daily average and the shape disappears. That subtraction is the part nobody designs for until they have lived it.
36	
37	## 4. Scope
38	
39	**In scope for v1**
40	
41	- The three-stage progression (section 5) and six behaviour tabs (section 6).
42	- Hardware presets: 1–2 GPUs (measured), Server A with 4 replicas, and Server B with 8 replicas (both extrapolated).
43	- A five-day simulated work week per tab.
44	- The Live / High-side telemetry toggle on every tab. Diagnosis from sparse telemetry is taught through this toggle.
45	
46	**Out of scope for v1**
47	
48	- Logging, authentication, session persistence.
49	- Autoscaling.
50	- Tensor parallelism: 1 GPU = 1 replica throughout.
51	- KV transfer or offload between GPUs or to CPU.
52	- Prefill/decode disaggregation.
53	- Role-based learning paths.
54	
55	**Candidate topics considered and deferred:**
56	
57	- Tokens, not requests, as the unit of load
58	- Sizing from users to concurrency to GPUs
59	- Model size, quantization, and parallelism layout
60	- Engine settings as capacity decisions
61	- Application design cost (prompts, RAG, agents, reasoning output)
62	- Mixed workloads and noisy neighbors
63	- Gray failure (slow but alive replicas)
64	- Planned maintenance at N−1
65	- Network-path effects on perceived latency
66	
67	## 5. Learning progression
68	
69	Design rule: each stage adds one new dimension and ends at the limit that motivates the next stage.
70	
71	| Stage | New dimension | Concepts introduced | Tabs | Ends at |
72	|---|---|---|---|---|
73	| a. 1 analyst, 1 GPU | None (baseline) | Tokens; prefill vs. decode; TTFT vs. TPOT; weights and KV share GPU memory; decode is memory-bandwidth bound | 1 | GPU mostly idle; one analyst can't use it |
74	| b. Many analysts, 1 GPU | Concurrency | Queueing; continuous batching; paged KV blocks; KV pool as the shared ceiling; chunked prefill; prefix caching and eviction; preemption and recompute; admission control | 2, 3 | Memory ceiling reached; the only fix is another GPU |
75	| c. Many analysts, many replicas | Placement | See below | 4, 5, 6 | — |
76	
77	Placement notes:
78	
79	- Paged attention belongs in stage (b). It is how one GPU divides its KV memory among concurrent requests, and prefix caching is built on the same blocks.
80	- Hot vs. cold cache first appears in (b): a returning session is hot if its blocks haven't been evicted. Stage (c) adds the question of which replica holds them.
81	
82	**Stage (c) concepts**
83	
84	1. **Replica independence.** Each GPU holds a full copy of the weights and its own KV pool. Compute and memory add up across replicas; cache contents do not.
85	2. **Session affinity and recompute cost.** A follow-up routed away from its history's replica prefills the entire conversation again. The cost grows with conversation length.
86	3. **Cache hit rate as effective capacity.** Every hit is prefill work not done. Under round-robin, a returning session lands on its original replica only about 1/N of the time.
87	4. **Shared prefixes.** A common system prompt is computed once per replica. It is a small cost, but it shows that cold replicas pay a warm-up cost.
88	5. **Locality vs. balance.** Affinity concentrates sessions and can overload a replica. Least-outstanding balances load but scatters sessions. Weighted scoring trades between the two.
89	6. **Load signals.** Request count ignores request cost. Signals are sampled with a delay, so a burst can pile onto the replica that looked idle.
90	7. **Hotspots and per-replica eviction.** One replica's KV can reach its ceiling and evict while the fleet as a whole has free memory.
91	8. **Fleet tail latency.** Fleet p99 is set by the worst replica. Imbalance shows in p99 before the mean moves.
92	9. **Replica loss.** Survivors absorb both the extra traffic and the recompute of lost histories. The latency impact exceeds the 1/N capacity loss.
93	10. **Remapping.** With mod-N hashing, losing one replica remaps most sessions: about 7/8 on 8 replicas, versus about 1/8 with consistent hashing.
94	11. **Rejoin.** A recovered replica has an empty cache and low load, so least-outstanding floods it with cold traffic. Recovery time is weight load plus warm-up.
95	
96	**Sub-progression within stage (c)**
97	
98	| Step | Content | Preset |
99	|---|---|---|
100	| c1 | 2 replicas, round-robin, single-turn requests. Capacity roughly doubles and routing looks irrelevant. | 2 replicas (measured) |
101	| c2 | Add multi-turn sessions. Round-robin now recomputes history. This is where routing starts to matter. | 2 replicas |
102	| c3 | Switch among the five routing policies. | 2 replicas |
103	| c4 | Hotspots, remapping, fleet p99. | Server A / B |
104	| c5 | Replica fail and rejoin, then the retry storm. | Server A / B |
105	
106	**Persona hooks for stage (c):**
107	
108	- **NetOps** already know sticky sessions and consistent hashing. The new fact is that breaking stickiness costs GPU time.
109	- **SysOps** own replica loss and rejoin.
110	- **PMs** see that N replicas do not deliver N× capacity.
111	- **FDEs** choose the routing policy.
112	
113	## 6. Tabs
114	
115	Each tab is bound to one hardware preset and resets to a known state (decision Q8). Only tabs 4 and 6 have a named fix (decision Q14). Full definitions are in 02-simulator, section 10.
116	
117	| Tab | Behaviour | Stage | Preset | Named fix |
118	|---|---|---|---|---|
119	| 1 | Long prompt: TTFT scales with prompt length, TPOT does not | a | 1 GPU | — |
120	| 2 | Saturation knee: p99 diverges from mean near capacity | b | 1 GPU | — |
121	| 3 | KV exhaustion: memory ceiling, preemption, recompute | b | 1 GPU | — |
122	| 4 | Routing policies | c | 2 replicas (measured) | Switch routing policy |
123	| 5 | Replica fail and recover | c | Server B, 8 replicas (proposed) | — |
124	| 6 | Retry storm | c | Server A, 4 replicas (proposed) | Backoff and admission control |
125	
126	## 7. Hardware presets
127	
128	| Preset | Replicas | Basis | On-screen label |
129	|---|---|---|---|
130	| Local | 1–2 | Measured on the author's 2×A100 server | Measured |
131	| Server A | 4 | Extrapolated from per-replica measurements | Extrapolated |
132	| Server B | 8 | Extrapolated from per-replica measurements | Extrapolated |
133	
134	The analyst population scales with replica count, so every preset runs at the same load per replica (decision Q9). Specifications and extrapolation limits are in 03-benchmarks.
135	
136	## 8. Telemetry model
137	
138	| Element | Live (default) | High side |
139	|---|---|---|
140	| Whose view | Omniscient | Off-site team (program office, vendor, FDE home team) |
141	| Canvas | Request dots, KV tanks, router | Feed goes quiet; tanks and dots hidden |
142	| Charts | Full time series | Daily bars for latency and utilization; KV panel reads "not collected on the high side" |
143	| Metrics | All | Requests served, mean end-to-end latency, mean nvidia-smi-style utilization. No error count. |
144	| Granularity | Per request and per replica | Daily, per replica |
145	| Latency of data | Immediate | Day N's rollup arrives at day N+1, 12:00 |
146	| Current day | Visible | Nothing until the rollup arrives |
147	
148	A failed replica shows up on the high side only indirectly, as a drop in that replica's requests served.
149	
150	## 9. Credibility
151	
152	- No accuracy claim (decision Q7). A footnote states that values are calibrated on 2×A100 with Llama 3.1 8B, and that shapes transfer to other hardware while absolute numbers do not.
153	- Extrapolated presets are labeled as extrapolated.
154	- Utilization shows both nvidia-smi-style utilization and compute utilization (decision Q2). The gap between them is part of lesson 3.
155	
156	## 10. Prior art and build rationale
157	
158	No existing tool combines a staged progression with queueing, KV-aware routing, outage scenarios, and a high-side telemetry view.
159	
160	| Tool | What it does | Gap relative to this product |
161	|---|---|---|
162	| BLIS (inference-sim/inference-sim) | Discrete-event cluster simulator with routing scorers, tiered KV, p99 TTFT/ITL/E2E; observe, replay, and calibrate against a live server | No documented replica crash or retry storm; no educational layer |
163	| Vidur (microsoft/vidur) | Profiled operators, config search, P90/P99 | Engine-fidelity research tool |
164	| LLMServingSim 2.0 | Cycle-level, heterogeneous hardware, p99 | Heavy build; hardware research focus |
165	| inference-lab (Doubleword) | vLLM facsimile with roofline; Rust/WASM | Optimistic; no failures |
166	| vllm-sr-sim (inference-fleet-sim) | Fleet sizing to a P99 TTFT SLO | Planning tool |
167	| NVIDIA AIConfigurator | Deployment config search | Steady-state estimates, no queueing tails |
168	| llm-d-inference-sim | Mock vLLM server | Emulation, not prediction |
169	| LLM Cluster Simulator (zhebrak) | Browser tool with Learn Mode (60 tasks) and RPG missions | Analytical parallelism, no serving dynamics |
170	| Modular LLM Inference Handbook tools | Single-concept visualizers | No system progression |
171	| BatchLab | Single-engine DES, p50/p95 | No replicas, no failures |
172	| Retry Storm Lab | Retry policy comparison | Generic, not LLM-specific |
173	
174	Patterns to borrow:
175	
176	- **BLIS** as an optional offline cross-check of engine output, and its cohort schema as a reference for the workload model.
177	- **BatchLab's** versioned scenario JSON as the pattern for resettable tab states.
178	- **Retry Storm Lab's** policy set (immediate, fixed, exponential, full jitter) and its metrics (amplification, peak demand, abandoned requests, recovery time).
179	- **Modular's** chunked-prefill visual for tab 1.
180	- **LLM Cluster Simulator's** WinningCriterion pattern, if goal-based challenges are ever added.
181	
182	## 11. Decision log (Theme 1)
183	
184	| ID | Question | Options considered | Decision |
185	|---|---|---|---|
186	| Q1 | How many replicas does v1 show? | Two only; two measured plus 4/8 extrapolated; slider 1–8 | Two measured plus 4 and 8 extrapolated |
187	| Q2 | What does "GPU has headroom" mean on screen? | Compute utilization; nvidia-smi utilization; both | Both; the gap is part of lesson 3 |
188	| Q3 | Which shared prefix drives the routing lesson? | Session history; shared document sets; both | Multi-turn session history |
189	| Q4 | Whose view is High side? | Off-site; on-site operator; three-way toggle | Off-site view |
190	| Q5 | How does an incident reach a daily rollup? | Full days at request level; incident window plus coarse day; compressed windows | Full days at request level, fast-forwarded (event-jumping) |
191	| Q6 | Where does diagnosis from sparse telemetry live? | Toggle only; start-in-High-side option; seventh tab | Toggle only |
192	| Q7 | Credibility claim? | None; measured-point overlays; validation page | No accuracy claim |
193	| Q8 | How do presets map to tabs? | Bound per tab; global selector; bound with override | Each tab bound to one preset |
194	| Q9 | Population across presets? | Scales with replicas; fixed; hand-set | Scales with replica count |
195	| Q10 | Session-aware routing mechanism? | Session-ID hashing; cache-aware matching; both | Session-ID hashing (enables the mod-N vs. consistent-hashing lesson) |
196	| Q11 | Which rollup metrics? | Latency type, utilization type, error count | Requests served, mean E2E latency, mean nvidia-smi utilization; no error count |
197	| Q12 | Rollup shape? | Daily vs. hourly; fleet vs. per replica; current-day handling | Daily cadence; per replica and 12-hour delivery lag (proposed, not objected to) |
198	| Q13 | Simulated horizon? | One day; work week; configurable | Work week, with the incident on one day |
199	| Q14 | Problem only, or problem and fix? | Observe only; fix on every tab; fix where it is the lesson | Fixes on tabs 4 and 6 only |
200	
201	## 12. Open items

204	2. **Presets for tabs 5 and 6.** Proposed: tab 5 on Server B (the remapping lesson is strongest at 8 replicas) and tab 6 on Server A (a 25% loss triggers the storm).
205	3. **Incident timing.** The day and time of each tab's incident within the week.

Tool reply2026-09-23T04:28:42.064Z
1	# Inference Simulator — 02 Simulator
2	
3	Status: scoping complete (September 2026).
4	Related docs: 01-product-and-rationale (lessons and scope), 03-benchmarks (calibration inputs), 04-stack (runtime).
5	
6	## 1. Principles
7	
8	- A pure TypeScript engine with no DOM access. Every random draw comes from a seeded stream.
9	- Discrete-event simulation with event-jumping: time jumps from one event to the next instead of ticking every engine step.
10	- The per-replica scheduler mirrors vLLM V1.
11	- Step duration comes from a roofline model with three calibrated numbers.
12	- The simulator knows every request's output length; the router does not.
13	
14	## 2. Fixed constants
15	
16	| Constant | Value | Source |
17	|---|---|---|
18	| Model | Llama 3.1 8B Instruct, FP16 | 03-benchmarks |
19	| Weights | ~16.06 GB (8.03B params × 2 bytes) | Computed |
20	| KV per token | 128 KiB (32 layers × 8 KV heads × 128 dim × 2 for K and V × 2 bytes) | Computed |
21	| KV pool per replica | Measured value from vLLM startup log (estimate ~140k tokens); shown to the user | 03-benchmarks R0 |
22	| KV block size | Measured engine value (vLLM default) | Run manifest |
23	| Max context length | 128k tokens (Llama 3.1 default; not capped) | Theme 3 Q2 |
24	| GPU | A100 PCIe 40GB, 250 W; peak dense FP16 312 TFLOPS; 1,555 GB/s | Spec sheet |
25	| Calibration | Compute efficiency η_c, bandwidth efficiency η_b, per-step overhead t_o | 03-benchmarks R1–R2 |
26	| Engine config | Chunked prefill on; prefix caching on; max_num_seqs and max_num_batched_tokens as benchmarked | Run manifest |
27	| Parallelism | TP=1; 1 GPU = 1 replica | Scope |
28	| Replicas per preset | 1, 2 (measured); 4 Server A, 8 Server B (extrapolated) | 01 §7 |
29	| Cold start | Replacement-host durations: weight load, engine init, ready | 03-benchmarks R7 |
30	| Router overhead | Fixed added latency constant | Hardware doc caveat |
31	| Horizon | 5-day work week per tab | Theme 1 Q13 |
32	
33	## 3. Request lifecycle
34	
35	```mermaid
36	stateDiagram-v2
37	  [*] --> Arrived
38	  Arrived --> Rejected: admission limit
39	  Arrived --> Waiting: admitted and dispatched
40	  Waiting --> Prefill: blocks allocated for uncached prompt
41	  Prefill --> Decode: first token
42	  Decode --> Finished: last output token
43	  Prefill --> Waiting: preempted
44	  Decode --> Waiting: preempted (recompute)
45	  Waiting --> Aborted: client timeout
46	  Prefill --> Aborted: client timeout
47	  Decode --> Aborted: client timeout
48	  Waiting --> Failed: replica crash
49	  Prefill --> Failed: replica crash
50	  Decode --> Failed: replica crash
51	  Rejected --> [*]
52	  Aborted --> [*]
53	  Failed --> [*]
54	  Finished --> [*]
55	```
56	
57	Rejected, Aborted, and Failed requests may spawn a Retry, which is a new Arrive after the backoff delay. The server aborts a request when its client times out (Theme 2 Q7).
58	
59	## 4. Events
60	
61	| Event | Trigger | Effect |
62	|---|---|---|
63	| Arrive | Open-loop session start, Next turn, or Retry | Request created at the router |
64	| Admit / Reject | Router admission control | Admitted requests are dispatched; rejected ones return an error, and retry policy applies |
65	| Dispatch | Routing policy | Request joins a replica's waiting queue |
66	| First token | Last prefill chunk completes | TTFT recorded; request enters decode |
67	| Finish | Final output token | E2E latency recorded; blocks released and kept in prefix cache as evictable |
68	| Preempt | A running request cannot get a KV block | Most recently admitted running request is freed and requeued |
69	| Evict | A free block holding cached content is reused | That history is lost from this replica; the session goes cold |
70	| Timeout | Client timeout elapses | Server aborts the request and frees its blocks; client may retry |
71	| Retry | Retry policy after Timeout, Reject, or Failed | New Arrive after backoff |
72	| Replica fail | Scenario trigger (crash) | In-flight requests fail at once; KV contents lost |
73	| Mark-down | Router detects the failure | Replica removed from the routing set |
74	| Replica load start / Ready | Recovery | Weight load, then engine init, then Ready; rejoins with an empty cache |
75	| Next turn | Think time elapses (see §8) | Arrive for the session's next turn |
76	
77	Internal events not shown to the user: prefill chunk boundary, KV-full threshold, metric bucket flush.
78	
79	Mapping from the draft list: Admin/reject became Admit/Reject; Prefill-complete became First token; Decode step became a computed quantity; "Prompt" was read as Preempt; Recover split into Load start and Ready. Evict, Timeout, Mark-down, and Next turn were added.
80	
81	## 5. Time advance: event-jumping
82	
83	Between events the batch composition on a replica is fixed. The engine computes when the next event occurs and advances directly to it. Candidates for the next event:
84	
85	- next arrival (pre-sampled open-loop schedule)
86	- next finish (output length known to the simulator)
87	- next prefill chunk completion
88	- KV pool exhaustion (free blocks ÷ blocks consumed per step)
89	- next client timeout
90	- scheduled failure or recovery phase
91	
92	Step duration grows as context grows, so the time for k decode steps is a sum over a growing series and is solved analytically. Token counts at any instant remain computable, so the canvas can animate per token.
93	
94	Expected scale: a Server B work week is on the order of 1M events, against tens of millions of engine steps.
95	
96	## 6. Step cost model (roofline, three calibrated numbers)
97	
98	```
99	compute_time = FLOPs / (η_c × 312 TFLOPS)
100	memory_time  = bytes / (η_b × 1,555 GB/s)
101	step_time    = t_o + max(compute_time, memory_time)
102	
103	FLOPs ≈ 2 × 8.03e9 × (prefill tokens + decode sequences) + attention FLOPs (∝ tokens × context)
104	bytes ≈ 16.06 GB weights + 128 KiB × (context tokens attended across the batch) + KV written
105	```
106	
107	- η_c is fitted to TTFT vs. prompt length (compute-bound prefill).
108	- η_b is fitted to batch-1 TPOT (memory-bound decode).
109	- t_o is the residual per-step overhead.
110	- The 250 W power cap is absorbed into η_c and η_b. It lowers compute throughput more than bandwidth, which is why one global factor was rejected (Theme 3 Q1).
111	
112	## 7. Scheduling rules
113	
114	### Per replica, per step (mirrors vLLM V1)
115	
116	1. **Admission to running.** A waiting request is scheduled when blocks for its uncached prompt tokens are free. Prefix-cache hits reduce the blocks needed. No blocks are reserved for future output.
117	2. **Step composition.** Running decodes go first, one token each. Prefill chunks fill the remaining token budget (max_num_batched_tokens), within the max_num_seqs cap.
118	3. **Block growth.** Decode allocates a new block each time a request crosses a block boundary.
119	4. **Preemption.** If a running request cannot get a block, the most recently admitted running request is preempted: its blocks are freed and it goes to the front of the waiting queue. It recomputes when rescheduled. Recompute is the only preemption mode.
120	5. **Prefix cache.** Blocks are identified by content hash. Freed blocks stay cached until reused, with LRU eviction among unreferenced blocks. A preempted request can therefore recover part of its prefill if its blocks were not reused.
121	
122	### Router (fleet level)
123	
124	- **Admission control** (tab 6 fix): a limit on requests queued at the router. Excess requests are rejected immediately.
125	- **Routing signals:** refreshed with a configurable delay, so bursts can pile onto a replica that looked idle.
126	
127	| Policy | Signal used | Notes |
128	|---|---|---|
129	| Round-robin | None | Ignores load |
130	| Least-outstanding | In-flight request count per replica | Counts requests, not cost |
131	| Session affinity (prefix-aware) | hash(session ID) → replica; scheme is mod-N or consistent hashing | Blind to eviction; the scheme parameter drives the remapping lesson |
132	| KV-utilization | Replica KV % | — |
133	| Weighted scoring | Weighted sum of affinity match, outstanding count, KV % | Weights are tunable |
134	
135	## 8. Load generator
136	
137	- **Arrivals:** open loop (Theme 2 Q4). Session starts follow a diurnal curve over analyst shift hours across a five-day work week.
138	- **Population:** analysts per replica × replica count (Theme 1 Q9).
139	- **Session turns:** turn N+1 is scheduled at turn N's arrival plus a think time. If turn N is still running, turn N+1 waits client-side until turn N completes. Load stays open-loop across the population, and each session stays coherent.
140	- **Session history:** turn N+1's prompt = system prompt + all prior user messages and outputs + the new message. Shared system prompt length is a parameter.
141	- **Distributions:** parametric (Theme 2 Q5):
142	  - new-message length and output length: lognormal
143	  - turns per session: geometric
144	  - think time: parametric distribution, family chosen during build
145	- **Client behavior:** timeout, retry policy (immediate, fixed, exponential, full jitter), and max retries.
146	- **Incident injection:** each tab's one-click trigger (§10).
147	
148	Default parameter values are set during the build and documented in code.
149	
150	## 9. Failure model
151	
152	- **Mode:** crash (Theme 2 Q6). Connections reset; in-flight requests fail immediately; clients retry per policy.
153	- **Detection:** Mark-down follows the crash after a short detection delay.
154	- **Recovery:** load start (weights from cold page cache), engine init (cold compile cache plus graph capture), then Ready. Durations are measured under replacement-host conditions (03-benchmarks R7). The replica rejoins with an empty KV cache. Under least-outstanding it receives a flood of cold traffic.
155	
156	## 10. Behaviours
157	
158	| Tab | Preset | Trigger | What changes visually | Lesson | Fix | Third chart |
159	|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
160	| 1 Long prompt | 1 GPU, 1 analyst | Send a long prompt next to short ones | The long request's dot stays in prefill color longer; TTFT jumps while TPOT stays flat | TTFT scales with prompt length; TPOT does not (prefill vs. decode) | — | Utilization (nvidia-smi vs. compute) |
161	| 2 Saturation knee | 1 GPU | Raise the arrival rate toward capacity | Waiting queue grows; mean latency rises slowly while p99 separates sharply | p99 diverges from the mean near capacity; averages hide it | — | Utilization |
162	| 3 KV exhaustion | 1 GPU | Long conversations and outputs | KV tank reaches ~98%; dots turn preempted color and return to the queue; preemption markers; nvidia-smi near 100% while compute stays low | The ceiling is memory; preemption recomputes paid-for prefill; throughput flat while p99 knees | — | Utilization |
163	| 4 Routing | 2 replicas | Switch routing policy | Tracked analyst's follow-ups land on another replica and re-prefill; TTFT rises; per-replica load shifts | Least-busy can be wrong; affinity trades imbalance for TTFT | Switch to session affinity or weighted | Per-replica load |
164	| 5 Replica fail and recover | Server B (proposed) | Crash a replica | Replica goes dark; its sessions re-dispatch and recompute; survivors' KV fills. On recovery: cold-start phases, empty tank, possible flood. Mod-N vs. consistent hashing changes how many sessions move. | Loss impact exceeds 1/N; cold start plus cold cache; remapping | — | Per-replica load |
165	| 6 Retry storm | Server A (proposed) | Crash or spike with short timeouts and immediate retries | Offered load climbs above organic arrivals; queues grow. With the fix: rejects appear, backoff spreads retries, the system recovers. | Timeouts and retries amplify outages; backoff and admission control contain them | Backoff with jitter plus admission control | Offered vs. admitted load |
166	
167	## 11. Metrics emitted
168	
169	**Per request:** request, session, analyst, and replica IDs; timestamps (arrive, admit, dispatch, first token, finish or abort); prompt tokens; cached tokens; output tokens; preemption count; retry count; outcome.
170	
171	**Per replica, per time bucket:**
172	
173	- KV usage %
174	- running and waiting counts
175	- preemptions
176	- prefix-cache hits and queries
177	- prefill and decode tokens/s
178	- nvidia-smi-style utilization (fraction of time a step is executing)
179	- compute utilization (achieved FLOPs ÷ peak)
180	- requests served
181	
182	**Fleet, per time bucket:** offered load, admitted load, rejects, retries, amplification factor.
183	
184	**Latency distributions:** TTFT, TPOT/ITL, and E2E stored as fixed log-spaced histograms per bucket (Theme 2 Q8). Histograms merge exactly to any zoom window, with about 1–2% error.
185	
186	**High-side rollup (derived):** per replica per day, requests served, mean E2E latency, and mean nvidia-smi-style utilization. Delivered 12 hours after the day closes.
187	
188	## 12. Determinism
189	
190	- **Seeded streams:** one PRNG stream per random source (arrivals, lengths, think times, failures, tie-breaks). Changing a policy never shifts arrival times, so policy comparisons are paired.
191	- **Repeatability scope:** the same seed in the same browser engine gives an identical run (Theme 4 Q5). `Math.exp` and `Math.log` are implementation-approximated in ECMAScript, so other engines may differ in the last bit.
192	
193	## 13. Decision log (Theme 2)
194	
195	| ID | Question | Options considered | Decision |
196	|---|---|---|---|
197	| — | Event list critique | Preempt, First token, and computed decode steps; add Evict, Timeout, Mark-down, Ready, Next turn | Accepted |
198	| Q1 | How does time advance? | Tick every step; event-jumping; hybrid | Event-jumping |
199	| Q2 | Step duration model? | Lookup tables; fitted linear; roofline with calibration | Roofline; refined in Theme 3 Q1 to three numbers (η_c, η_b, t_o) |
200	| Q3 | Scheduler fidelity? | Mirror vLLM V1; simplified reservation; V1 with toggles | Mirror vLLM V1 |
201	| Q4 | Arrival model? | Open loop; closed-loop sessions; hybrid | Open loop, with the session-coherence rule in §8 |
202	| Q5 | Length and think-time source? | Parametric; empirical trace; parametric fitted to a trace | Parametric |
203	| Q6 | Failure mode? | Crash; hang; both | Crash |
204	| Q7 | Work after client timeout? | Abort on disconnect; keep generating; toggle | Abort on disconnect (vLLM behavior) |
205	| Q8 | Percentiles across zoom levels? | Exact from records; mergeable histograms; fixed windows | Fixed log-spaced histograms per bucket |
206	
207	## 14. Open items
208	
209	1. Default values for all load-generator distributions and client timeout and retry parameters.
210	2. Form of the admission limit (queued requests at the router vs. per-replica in-flight cap) and its default.
211	3. Default router signal refresh delay and crash detection delay.
212	4. Incident day and time per tab (shared with 01, open item 3).

1	# Inference Simulator — 03 Benchmarks
2	
3	Status: scoping complete (September 2026).
4	Related docs: 02-simulator (how the results are used), 06-deployment (repo layout).
5	
6	## 1. Purpose
7	
8	- Calibrate the three roofline numbers: compute efficiency η_c, bandwidth efficiency η_b, and per-step overhead t_o.
9	- Measure the KV pool size and engine limits the simulator uses as constants.
10	- Measure cold-start durations for replica recovery.
11	- Validate that the simulator reproduces the saturation knee.
12	- Commit raw data so anyone can check the calibration.
13	
14	## 2. System under test
15	
16	| Component | Configuration | Relevance |
17	|---|---|---|
18	| CPUs | 2 × Intel Xeon Platinum 8380, 40 cores each (80 cores / 160 threads), 2.30 GHz base, 3.40 GHz max, AVX-512 and VNNI | Tokenization, scheduling, host transfers |
19	| System RAM | 1 TiB (16 × 64 GiB DDR4 ECC RDIMM, 3,200 MT/s, 8 DIMMs per socket) | Page cache for weights |
20	| GPUs | 2 × NVIDIA A100 PCIe 40GB, compute capability 8.0, ECC on, MIG off | One replica each |
21	| GPU interconnect | No NVLink; PCIe 4.0 ×16 each; topology SYS (crosses the socket interconnect) | No effect at TP=1 |
22	| NUMA | 2 nodes, one GPU per node | Pin each replica NUMA-local |
23	| GPU power | 250 W per GPU; power limiting observed, no thermal slowdown | Absorbed into η_c and η_b |
24	| Storage | 3.84 TB Intel NVMe (root, LVM/ext4); 2 TB Samsung 990 PRO NVMe at /data | Weight loading, cold start |
25	| Network | 2 × Intel X550 10GbE; active port negotiates 100 Mb/s | Run the load generator locally |
26	| Software | Ubuntu 24.04.2 LTS, kernel 6.8.0-134, NVIDIA driver 580.159.04, vLLM 0.20.1, PyTorch 2.11.0, CUDA runtime 13.0 | Record in every run manifest |
27	
28	## 3. Model and engine
29	
30	- **Model:** Llama 3.1 8B Instruct, FP16. The Instruct variant fits the chat workload; KV and compute costs are identical to the base model.
31	- **Engine:** vLLM, TP=1, one instance per GPU, prefix caching on, chunked prefill on.
32	- **Context:** max_model_len 131,072 (Theme 3 Q2). This allows one request to consume most of the KV pool, which is itself a lesson.
33	- **Memory:** default gpu_memory_utilization (0.9).
34	
35	KV arithmetic:
36	
37	- Weights: 8.03B params × 2 bytes ≈ 16 GB.
38	- KV per token: 32 layers × 8 KV heads × 128 head dim × 2 (K and V) × 2 bytes = 128 KiB.
39	- KV pool: about 18 GiB remains after weights and activations, on the order of 140k tokens per GPU. That is about 17 concurrent 8k-token sessions, or about 4 at 32k. Record the exact value vLLM reports at startup.
40	
41	Why this model:
42	
43	- It fits on one A100 40GB with room for KV, so 1 GPU = 1 replica holds throughout and tensor parallelism stays out of v1.
44	- The KV ceiling is reachable with realistic workloads.
45	- It uses grouped-query attention, like most current production models.
46	
47	Limitation: high-side deployments often run 70B-class models, which need tensor parallelism. Shapes transfer (the knee, the memory ceiling, routing effects), but absolute numbers do not. A larger model on the same GPU hits the memory ceiling sooner.
48	
49	## 4. Hardware presets and extrapolation
50	
51	Principle: benchmarks measure one replica. The presets change only the replica count and keep everything that sets per-replica behavior identical: GPU SKU and power cap, TP=1, one engine per GPU, NUMA-local placement, ×16 PCIe per GPU, and the same CPU family.
52	
53	| | Current system | Server A | Server B |
54	|---|---|---|---|
55	| Real chassis class | — | 2U, Dell PowerEdge R750xa class (dual 3rd Gen Xeon, up to 4 double-width PCIe GPUs) | 4U, Supermicro SYS-420GP-TNR class (dual 3rd Gen Xeon, up to 10 double-width GPUs, PCIe 4.0 switches, dual-root) |
56	| CPUs | 2 × Xeon Platinum 8380 | Same | Same |
57	| GPUs | 2 × A100 PCIe 40GB, 250 W | 4 × same | 8 × same |
58	| GPUs per NUMA node | 1 | 2 | 4 |
59	| GPU attach | CPU-direct ×16 | CPU-direct ×16 | ×16 per GPU behind PCIe switches (shared uplinks) |
60	| CPU cores per GPU | 40 | 20 | 10 |
61	| System RAM | 1 TiB (512 GiB/GPU) | 1 TiB (256 GiB/GPU) | 2 TiB (256 GiB/GPU) |
62	| KV pool (estimate) | 2 × ~140k tokens | 4 × ~140k | 8 × ~140k |
63	| GPU power | 500 W | 1,000 W | 2,000 W (chassis PSUs: 4 × 2,000 W redundant) |
64	| Capacity lost per replica failure | 50% | 25% | 12.5% |
65	
66	**Where extrapolation holds:** per-replica TTFT vs. prompt length, TPOT vs. batch size, KV pool size, onset of preemption, and cold start of one replica rejoining.
67	
68	**Caveats:**
69	
70	- **Simultaneous cold starts** share storage and, on Server B, PCIe switch uplinks. Only single-replica rejoin matches the benchmark.
71	- **PCIe switches** affect weight loading only; steady-state decode at TP=1 barely touches PCIe.
72	- **Other GPU variants** (A100 SXM at 400 W, 80GB cards) would change per-GPU numbers.
73	- **Router and network overhead** are not measured; the simulator adds a fixed constant.
74	- **Weight transfer to a replacement host** is excluded: weights are assumed pre-staged on local disk. Pulling 16 GB across the enclave network would add about 15 s at 10GbE or about 2 min at 1GbE.
75	
76	## 5. Harness
77	
78	- **Load:** `vllm bench serve`.
79	  - Datasets: random with fixed input and output lengths (EOS ignored), random with a fixed shared prefix, and prefix_repetition.
80	  - Arrivals: Poisson or gamma request rates, or a max-concurrency cap; linear or exponential ramp-up.
81	  - Output: `--save-result --save-detailed` for per-request JSON.
82	- **Wrapper script:**
83	  - Starts and stops vLLM and timestamps startup phases from log lines.
84	  - Scrapes `/metrics` every second.
85	  - Samples NVML every second: power, SM and memory clocks, utilization.gpu, memory used, and clock-event reasons.
86	  - Writes a run manifest.
87	- **Practices:**
88	  - Pin each vLLM instance to its GPU's NUMA node (CPU and memory binding).
89	  - Run the load generator on the same host, so the 100 Mb/s link stays out of the measurements.
90	  - Log power and clocks so power-limited intervals are visible.
91	
92	Example skeletons (all values are placeholders):
93	
94	```bash
95	CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=0 numactl --cpunodebind=0 --membind=0 \
96	  vllm serve meta-llama/Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct --dtype float16 \
97	  --tensor-parallel-size 1 --max-model-len 131072 --enable-prefix-caching --port 8001
98	
99	vllm bench serve --model meta-llama/Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct --port 8001 \
100	  --dataset-name random --random-input-len 2048 --random-output-len 256 --ignore-eos \
101	  --request-rate 4 --num-prompts 500 \
102	  --percentile-metrics ttft,tpot,itl,e2el --metric-percentiles 50,90,99 \
103	  --save-result --save-detailed --result-dir benchmarks/raw/<run-id>
104	```
105	
106	## 6. Run plan
107	
108	| Run | Purpose | Method | Feeds |
109	|---|---|---|---|
110	| R0 Startup | KV pool size, engine limits | Record reported KV cache size (tokens), max concurrency, block size, max_num_seqs, max_num_batched_tokens | Constants |
111	| R1 Prefill calibration | η_c | Batch-1 TTFT vs. prompt length, synthetic fixed lengths from 128 up to ~120k tokens | Cost model |
112	| R2 Decode calibration | η_b, t_o | Batch-1 TPOT at short context; TPOT vs. concurrency; TPOT vs. context length at fixed concurrency | Cost model |
113	| R3 Knee validation | Check simulated knee | Open-loop stepped request rates with sampled (lognormal) lengths, KV below ceiling, each step held to steady state | Validation (internal) |
114	| R4 KV exhaustion | Preemption behavior | Long outputs at a rate that fills the pool; capture preemptions, KV %, waiting | Validation (internal) |
115	| R5 Sustained overload | Steady-state thrash | Overload held for several minutes; waiting-queue growth, preemption rate | Validation (internal) |
116	| R6 Prefix cache | Warm vs. cold TTFT | prefix_repetition (cached prefix plus new suffix) vs. unique prefixes. In KV terms, a follow-up turn is a cached prefix plus a new suffix. | Cache model check |
117	| R7 Cold start | Recovery durations | Three conditions: process restart (caches warm); host reboot (drop page cache); replacement host (drop page cache and move the vLLM compile cache aside). Phases: weights loaded, engine ready, first request. | Recovery constants (replacement host) |
118	| R8 Independence | Validate extrapolation | Both GPUs at full load together vs. each solo | Server A/B validity |
119	
120	Replacement-host emulation: `sync; echo 3 | sudo tee /proc/sys/vm/drop_caches`, then move vLLM's compile cache directory (by default under `~/.cache/vllm`) aside before start. Graph capture reruns on every start regardless.
121	
122	## 7. Metrics captured
123	
124	| Source | Metrics |
125	|---|---|
126	| bench serve (per request) | Prompt and output tokens, TTFT, TPOT, ITL, E2E, success or error |
127	| bench serve (summary) | Throughput and latency percentiles (50/90/99) |
128	| vLLM `/metrics` (1 s) | `vllm:kv_cache_usage_perc`, `vllm:num_preemptions`, `vllm:prefix_cache_hits`, `vllm:prefix_cache_queries`, `vllm:num_requests_running`, `vllm:num_requests_waiting`, `vllm:iteration_tokens_total`, `vllm:e2e_request_latency_seconds` (counters may appear with a `_total` suffix) |
129	| NVML (1 s) | utilization.gpu, power draw, SM and memory clocks, memory used, clock-event reasons |
130	| Wrapper | Startup phase timestamps, run manifest |
131	
132	## 8. Output format
133	
134	Raw results and scrapes are committed (Theme 3 Q6). A script derives a small calibration file that the app imports at build time.
135	
136	```
137	benchmarks/
138	  scripts/                 # wrapper, derivation script
139	  raw/<run-id>/
140	    manifest.json          # versions, hardware, engine args, run type
141	    bench.json             # vllm bench serve --save-detailed output
142	    metrics.jsonl          # 1 s /metrics scrapes
143	    nvml.jsonl             # 1 s NVML samples
144	  derived/calibration.json # consumed by the app
145	```
146	
147	`calibration.json` shape (values are placeholders until the runs complete):
148	
149	```json
150	{
151	  "schemaVersion": 1,
152	  "source": { "runIds": [], "vllm": "0.20.1", "gpu": "A100-PCIe-40GB", "powerLimitW": 250 },
153	  "model": { "name": "Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct", "dtype": "float16", "weightBytes": null, "kvBytesPerToken": 131072 },
154	  "engine": { "kvPoolTokens": null, "blockSize": null, "maxNumSeqs": null, "maxNumBatchedTokens": null, "maxModelLen": 131072 },
155	  "costModel": { "computeEfficiency": null, "bandwidthEfficiency": null, "stepOverheadMs": null },
156	  "coldStartMs": {
157	    "processRestart": { "weightsLoaded": null, "engineReady": null },
158	    "hostReboot": { "weightsLoaded": null, "engineReady": null },
159	    "replacementHost": { "weightsLoaded": null, "engineReady": null }
160	  },
161	  "prefixCache": { "warmTtftMs": null, "coldTtftMs": null, "prefixTokens": null }
162	}
163	```

169	| Q1 | How many calibration factors? | One global factor; compute and bandwidth efficiency plus overhead; prefill table plus roofline decode | η_c, η_b, and t_o (supersedes the single factor from Theme 2 Q2) |
170	| Q2 | Maximum context length? | Leave 128k; cap at 32k; benchmark 128k and expose the cap | Leave 128k |
171	| Q3 | Synthetic or realistic lengths? | Synthetic only; realistic only; synthetic for calibration plus a realistic knee validation | Synthetic for calibration plus one open-loop sampled-length knee run |
172	| Q4 | Which cold state does recovery represent? | Process restart; host reboot; replacement host; measure all and parameterize | Replacement host (all three measured) |
173	| Q5 | Overload run structure? | One ramp; separate knee and KV runs; both plus sustained overload | Knee run, KV run, and sustained overload |

1	# Inference Simulator — 04 Stack
2	
3	Status: scoping complete (September 2026).
4	Related docs: 02-simulator (engine design), 05-ui-ux (what is rendered), 06-deployment (hosting and CSP).
5	
6	## 1. Stack summary
7	
8	| Layer | Choice | Notes |
9	|---|---|---|
10	| App | React + Vite + TypeScript | Static SPA |
11	| Styling | Tailwind CSS | Also handles simple CSS transitions |
12	| UI animation | Motion (MIT) | Modal, tabs, sidebar, drawer transitions |
13	| Simulator pane | Canvas 2D with a `requestAnimationFrame` loop | Draws the state at the playhead; pure function of simulation time |
14	| Charts | d3 for scales, axes, and path generation; React renders SVG | Stated exception to the no-SVG rule, which applies to the simulator pane |
15	| Chart transitions | d3 transitions on chart data | Includes the Live to High-side collapse |
16	| Simulation engine | Pure TypeScript, no DOM, seeded PRNG streams | Main thread; see §3 |
17	| Tests | Vitest unit tests per module | — |
18	| Lint and format | ESLint + Prettier | Rules in §5 |
19	
20	**Not used:** three.js, GSAP, a Python backend, client-side routing, localStorage, sessionStorage, runtime network calls.
21	
22	## 2. Why not GSAP
23	
24	The draft preferred GSAP unless something did the job better. Three things need animating here, and none needs GSAP:
25	
26	1. **Canvas dots and KV tanks** must be a pure function of simulation time, because playback pauses, scrubs, forks, and changes speed. Tween libraries run on wall-clock time and drift from the simulation. A rAF loop that draws the state at the playhead needs no library.
27	2. **The chart collapse** from Live to High side is a transition on the chart's own data, which d3 handles.
28	3. **UI chrome** needs simple fades and slides. Motion covers this declaratively in React.
29	
30	GSAP's strengths (timelines, scroll-triggered sequencing) go unused. Its license is also proprietary: free under Webflow's standard no-charge license, which allows use on any website or app but is not OSI open source. A proprietary dependency is one more review item if anyone later clears the app for use inside an enclave.
31	
32	## 3. Architecture
33	
34	```mermaid
35	flowchart LR
36	  CAL[calibration.json<br/>build-time import] --> ENG
37	  CTRL[Controls: trigger, params, toggle] -->|fork at playhead| ENG[Engine<br/>pure TS, seeded]
38	  ENG --> RES[Results<br/>histogram buckets, request records, events]
39	  RES --> PB[Playback store<br/>playhead, speed]
40	  PB --> CAN[Canvas rAF loop]
41	  PB --> CH[Charts: d3 + React SVG]
42	  PB --> SB[Sidebar: text, live status, rollup table]
43	```
44	
45	- **Where the engine runs (Q1):** on the main thread. At tab load it computes the full work week before playback. Results are fixed per run, which makes scrubbing trivial.
46	- **Mid-week changes (Q2):** fork at the playhead. The engine snapshots its state at time t, applies the change, and recomputes from t to the end of the week. The past before t is kept, and charts mark the fork time. Engine state must be plain data (queues, block tables, RNG positions) so `structuredClone` can copy it.
47	- **Fallback:** if a Server B recompute blocks the UI noticeably, move the engine into a Web Worker. Because the engine has no DOM access and its state is plain data, the move is contained.
48	- **Chart density:** draw at most one bucket per pixel column, so SVG paths stay small at every zoom level.
49	- **Determinism (Q5):** the same seed in the same browser engine gives an identical run. Each random source has its own stream (see 02-simulator §12).
50	
51	## 4. Proposed source layout
52	
53	```
54	src/
55	  engine/        # pure TS: events, scheduler, router, cost model, load generator, metrics
56	  engine/rng/    # seeded PRNG streams
57	  playback/      # playback store, fork handling
58	  sim-view/      # canvas renderer
59	  charts/        # d3 scales, React SVG charts
60	  ui/            # tabs, toolbar, drawer, sidebar, modal, tooltips
61	  scenarios/     # per-tab definitions: preset, seed, trigger, parameters, sidebar text
62	  data/          # calibration.json (copied from benchmarks/derived)
63	```
64	
65	## 5. Code and runtime rules
66	
67	| Rule | Enforcement |
68	|---|---|
69	| Small files | Review; lint max-lines threshold set during build |
70	| Named exports only | `import/no-default-export`, with an override for React lazy-boundary files |
71	| No runtime network calls | `no-restricted-globals` for `fetch`, `XMLHttpRequest`, `WebSocket`, `EventSource`; CSP `connect-src 'none'` (06-deployment) |
72	| No browser storage | `no-restricted-globals` for `localStorage`, `sessionStorage`, `indexedDB` |
73	| No client-side routing | No router dependency |
74	| Seeded randomness only | `no-restricted-properties` for `Math.random` in `src/engine` |
75	| No DOM in engine | Lint boundary: `src/engine` may not import from UI folders or reference `window` or `document` |
76	| No three.js | Dependency review |
77	| No SVG in simulator pane | Review; charts are the stated exception |
78	
79	## 6. Decision log (Theme 4)
80	
81	| ID | Question | Options considered | Decision |
82	|---|---|---|---|
83	| Q1 | Where does the engine run? | Main thread, full week at load; Worker, full week at load; Worker streaming ahead | Main thread, full week at load (Worker as fallback) |
84	| Q2 | Mid-week parameter changes? | Restart from t=0 and seek; fork at playhead; edit only while paused | Fork at playhead |
85	| Q3 | Animation library? | GSAP; Motion; none | Motion for UI chrome; rAF canvas; d3 chart transitions |
86	| Q4 | Chart rendering? | d3 math plus React SVG; d3-owned DOM; canvas series | d3 math plus React SVG |
87	| Q5 | What does "repeatable" promise? | Same engine; cross-engine via pure-JS math; same engine plus shareable seeds | Same seed, same browser engine |
88	| Q6 | Testing beyond unit tests? | Unit only; plus golden runs; plus property tests | Unit tests only |
89	
90	## 7. Open items
91	
92	1. Measure Server B week compute time and fork recompute time on the main thread early; set the threshold for moving to a Worker.
93	2. Because testing is unit-only (Q6), the event-jumping time calculations are the highest-risk code. Give them focused unit tests.

1	# Inference Simulator — 05 UI/UX
2	
3	Status: scoping complete (September 2026).
4	Related docs: 01-product-and-rationale (tabs, telemetry model), 02-simulator (behaviours, metrics), 04-stack (rendering).
5	
6	## 1. Layout
7	
8	The simulator area takes 70% of the width and the sidebar takes 30%. The simulator area stacks the tabs, toolbar, canvas, three charts, and the week timeline.
9	
10	```
11	+-----------------------------------------------------+----------------------+
12	| Tabs: 1 Long prompt | 2 Knee | 3 KV | 4 Routing |   |                      |
13	|       5 Fail/recover | 6 Retry storm                |  Sidebar (30%)       |
14	+-----------------------------------------------------+  - Tab explanation   |
15	| Toolbar: [Trigger] [Live | High side] [Play] [Speed] |  - Live status line  |
16	|          [Parameters drawer]                        |  - Rollup table      |
17	+-----------------------------------------------------+    (High side)       |
18	| Canvas: router -> replicas (KV tanks, request dots) |  - Footnote:         |
19	+-----------------------------------------------------+    calibration,      |
20	| Chart 1: latency (mean vs p99)                      |    no accuracy claim |
21	| Chart 2: memory (KV %, preemption markers)          |                      |
22	| Chart 3: per tab                                    |                      |
23	|   (shared time axis, vertically aligned)            |                      |
24	+-----------------------------------------------------+                      |
25	| Week timeline: scrub, incident and fork markers     |                      |
26	+-----------------------------------------------------+----------------------+
27	```
28	
29	## 2. Intro modal
30	
31	Appears on every page load. It is dismissible and has no "don't show again" persistence. Target under ~120 words plus a "Start exploring" button.
32	
33	Draft text (about 75 words):
34	
35	> **Inference Simulator**
36	> An interactive model of serving a large language model inside a fixed-capacity enclave: a set number of GPUs, a known analyst population, and no way to add hardware when load spikes.
37	> Built for the engineers, operators, and program staff who run LLM systems on constrained, high-side networks.
38	> Calibrated on Llama 3.1 8B running on two NVIDIA A100 40GB GPUs; larger configurations are extrapolated.
39	> The tabs are a teaching order, and every parameter is adjustable.
40	> **[Start exploring]**
41	
42	## 3. Tabs
43	
44	- There are six tabs in teaching order. Each is bound to one hardware preset and resets to a known state: preset, seed, parameters, and tracked analyst.
45	- Extrapolated presets (Server A, Server B) carry an "Extrapolated" label.
46	- Tabs 4 and 6 expose a named fix; the others are observe-and-tweak.
47	
48	## 4. Toolbar and parameters drawer (Q5)
49	
50	- **Always visible:** the tab's one-click trigger, the Live / High-side toggle, play/pause, and speed.
51	- **Collapsible drawer:** tab-specific parameters, such as routing policy and hash scheme, arrival rate, timeout and retry policy, and admission limit.
52	- Changing a parameter mid-week forks the run at the playhead (04-stack §3). The charts and timeline mark the fork time.
53	
54	## 5. Playback and timeline (Q1)
55	
56	- **Speed control:** 1× to 1000×.
57	- **Week timeline:** a week-long, scrubbable timeline with incident markers and fork markers.
58	- **Speed threshold:** above it, the canvas hides individual dots and shows aggregate flow per replica, because dots are only legible at about 1–10×.
59	
60	## 6. Charts (Q2)
61	
62	Three charts are stacked with one shared time axis and vertically aligned, so the same vertical slice is the same moment.
63	
64	| Chart | Content | Tabs |
65	|---|---|---|
66	| 1 Latency (fixed) | Mean vs. p99 E2E latency, plus TTFT p99 | All |
67	| 2 Memory (fixed) | KV cache % with preemption markers | All |
68	| 3 Per tab | Utilization: nvidia-smi-style vs. compute | 1–3 |
69	| | Per-replica load | 4–5 |
70	| | Offered vs. admitted load (retry amplification) | 6 |
71	
72	- **Multiple replicas:** a fleet line with the worst replica highlighted, which stays readable at 8 replicas.
73	- **Rendering:** React SVG with d3 scales, drawing at most one bucket per pixel column.
74	- **Percentiles:** computed from merged histograms, so any zoom level is exact to within about 1–2%.
75	
76	## 7. Canvas encoding (Q6)
77	
78	- **Layout:** the router on the left, replicas on the right. Each replica shows a KV tank (fill = KV %) and its request dots.
79	- **Dot colors by state:** queued, prefill, decode, preempted. Use a colorblind-safe palette with a secondary cue (shape or outline) for preempted.
80	- **Tracked analyst:**
81	  - Each tab starts with a seed-chosen analyst whose session spans the tab's incident.
82	  - Their requests are traced across replicas and labeled with TTFT.
83	  - Clicking any dot switches tracking to that analyst.
84	- **Replica states:** Ready, Down (dark), and Loading (weight load, then engine init). A rejoining replica shows an empty tank.
85	
86	## 8. Sidebar (Q4)
87	
88	- **Static text per tab:** what to watch and why.
89	- **Live status line:** generated from engine state through message templates, e.g. "Replica 3 is preempting; KV at 98%" or "Replica 5 is loading weights."
90	- **Rollup table** (High side only): days × replicas × three metrics.
91	- **Footnote:** calibration basis and no accuracy claim.
92	
93	## 9. Telemetry toggle (Q3)
94	
95	| Element | Live (default) | High side |
96	|---|---|---|
97	| Canvas | Dots, tanks, router activity | Feed goes quiet; tanks and dots hidden; replica outlines remain |
98	| Chart 1 | Time series | Daily bars per replica (mean E2E latency) |
99	| Chart 2 | KV % time series | Empty panel: "not collected on the high side" |
100	| Chart 3 | Tab-specific series | Daily bars per replica for utilization (nvidia-smi-style); other tab-specific series become empty panels |
101	| Sidebar | Text and live status | Text plus rollup table; the live status line is hidden |
102	| Timing | Immediate | Day N's rollup appears once the playhead passes day N+1, 12:00; the current day is empty |
103	
104	The toggle is available on every tab. Switching back to Live restores the full view of the same run, which shows the subtraction directly.
105	
106	## 10. Tooltips
107	
108	Tooltips are orientational only (what a control or element is). Scenarios and the sidebar do the teaching.
109	
110	## 11. Decision log (Theme 5)
111	
112	| ID | Question | Options considered | Decision |
113	|---|---|---|---|
114	| Q1 | Moving through a work week? | Speed control plus timeline; auto-pacing per tab; focus-and-context window | Speed control (1×–1000×) plus scrubbable timeline with incident markers |
115	| Q2 | Which three charts? | Fixed three; two fixed plus per-tab third; user-selectable | Latency and memory fixed; third chart per tab |
116	| Q3 | Charts in High-side mode? | Keep slots with empty panels; collapse to table; both | Both: daily bars and empty panels, plus rollup table in sidebar |
117	| Q4 | How does the sidebar teach? | Static text; stepped narrative; static plus live status | Static text plus live status line |
118	| Q5 | Where do controls live? | Sidebar; toolbar; toolbar plus drawer | Toolbar for trigger, toggle, and playback; drawer for parameters |
119	| Q6 | Canvas encoding? | Uniform dots; colored by state; state plus tracked analyst | Colored by state plus a tracked analyst |
120	
121	Carried from the draft: the 70/30 split, the intro modal on every load without persistence, orientational tooltips, and three stacked charts on a shared time axis.
122	
123	## 12. Open items
124	
125	1. Minimum supported viewport (not discussed; propose desktop-first, with a notice below a width set during build).
126	2. Palette and secondary encodings for dot states and replica states.
127	3. The set of live status line templates per tab.
128	4. Speed threshold at which dots switch to aggregate flow.

1	# Inference Simulator — 06 Deployment and CI/CD
2	
3	Status: scoping complete (September 2026).
4	Related docs: 04-stack (runtime rules the CSP enforces), 03-benchmarks (repo layout for data).
5	
6	## 1. Summary
7	
8	- Static SPA with no backend and no runtime secrets.
9	- Hosted on AWS: S3 behind CloudFront, ACM certificate, Route 53 DNS.
10	- The site lives at a subdomain of yoschwab.com (subdomain to be provided). The yoschwab.com zone is in Route 53 in the same AWS account.
11	- Infrastructure is Terraform. CI applies it on push to `prod`.
12	- GitHub Actions deploys on push to `prod` (a merge or a direct commit). Pull requests into `prod` build and test only.
13	- No release pipeline.
14	- Public GitHub repo. No secrets in any generated file, and secrets are never requested in conversation.
15	
16	## 2. Architecture
17	
18	```mermaid
19	flowchart LR
20	  DEV[Author machine] -->|push / PR| GH[GitHub public repo]
21	  GH -->|pull_request to prod| CI[CI: lint, typecheck, test, build]
22	  GH -->|push to prod| DEP[Deploy workflow]
23	  DEP -->|OIDC| ROLE[AWS deploy role]
24	  ROLE --> TF[Terraform apply: infra/site]
25	  ROLE --> SYNC[S3 sync + CloudFront invalidation]
26	  TF --> S3[(Site bucket, private)]
27	  TF --> CF[CloudFront + OAC + headers policy]
28	  TF --> ACM[ACM cert, us-east-1]
29	  TF --> R53[Route 53 alias record]
30	  TF --> LOGS[(Log bucket, 90-day lifecycle)]
31	  CF --> S3
32	  CF --> LOGS
33	```
34	
35	## 3. Terraform layout
36	
37	**`infra/bootstrap/`** — applied locally, once. CI depends on these resources, so they must exist before any workflow runs.
38	
39	- GitHub OIDC identity provider.
40	- Deploy role. Trust is scoped to the `prod` branch: subject `repo:<owner>/<repo>:ref:refs/heads/prod`, audience `sts.amazonaws.com`.
41	- Deploy role permissions: the site, log, and state buckets; CloudFront; ACM; and the Route 53 zone. No IAM write, because IAM stays in the bootstrap stack.
42	- State bucket: versioning, encryption, public access blocked.
43	- The bootstrap stack's own state stays local and out of the repo.
44	
45	**`infra/site/`** — applied by CI on push to `prod`.
46	
47	- Backend: S3 with native locking (`use_lockfile = true`, Terraform 1.10+). No DynamoDB table.
48	- Site bucket: private, accessed only through CloudFront Origin Access Control.
49	- Log bucket: lifecycle rule deleting objects after 90 days.
50	- CloudFront distribution:
51	  - default root object `index.html`
52	  - HTTPS redirect
53	  - cache behaviors per §6
54	  - response headers policy per §5
55	- ACM certificate in us-east-1 (required for CloudFront), with DNS validation records in Route 53.
56	- Route 53 alias A and AAAA records for the subdomain.
57	
58	## 4. GitHub Actions
59	
60	| Workflow | Trigger | Permissions | Steps |
61	|---|---|---|---|
62	| `ci.yml` | `pull_request` targeting `prod` | `contents: read` (no AWS) | Install, lint, typecheck, test, build |
63	| `deploy.yml` | `push` to `prod` | `id-token: write`, `contents: read` | Install, lint, typecheck, test, build → assume role via OIDC → `terraform init/plan/apply` (infra/site) → `aws s3 sync` with cache headers → CloudFront invalidation of `/index.html` |
64	
65	- `deploy.yml` uses a concurrency group so deploys never overlap.
66	- Third-party actions are pinned to full commit SHAs.
67	- Fork PRs receive no OIDC tokens, so CI for them runs without AWS access regardless of configuration.
68	
69	**Account-specific values** (Q5: GitHub secrets). The author sets these; the repo never contains them.
70	
71	| Secret | Contents |
72	|---|---|
73	| `AWS_DEPLOY_ROLE_ARN` | Role ARN output by the bootstrap stack |
74	| `TF_STATE_BUCKET` | State bucket name |
75	
76	Setup commands, run on the author's machine (placeholders only; `gh secret set` prompts for the value):
77	
78	```bash
79	gh repo create <owner>/<repo> --public --source . --push
80	git push -u origin prod
81	gh secret set AWS_DEPLOY_ROLE_ARN
82	gh secret set TF_STATE_BUCKET
83	```
84	
85	`.gitignore` covers `*.tfstate*`, `.terraform/`, and `*.tfvars` (an example file with placeholders is committed).
86	
87	## 5. Security headers
88	
89	A CloudFront response headers policy provides the browser-level backstop for the no-network and no-storage rules. Proposed CSP:
90	
91	```
92	default-src 'self'; script-src 'self'; style-src 'self'; img-src 'self' data:;
93	font-src 'self'; connect-src 'none'; worker-src 'self'; object-src 'none';
94	base-uri 'self'; form-action 'none'; frame-ancestors 'none'
95	```
96	
97	- `connect-src 'none'` blocks any runtime network call from the page.
98	- `worker-src 'self'` covers the Worker fallback in 04-stack.
99	- Fonts are self-hosted; no third-party font CDN.
100	- Test `style-src 'self'` against the production build. If a dependency injects `<style>` elements, allow them by hash, or by `'unsafe-inline'` for styles only.
101	- Other headers:
102	  - HSTS: one year, including subdomains
103	  - `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff`
104	  - `Referrer-Policy: no-referrer`
105	  - a minimal `Permissions-Policy`
106	
107	## 6. Caching
108	
109	| Path | Cache-Control | On deploy |
110	|---|---|---|
111	| `/assets/*` (Vite hashed) | `public, max-age=31536000, immutable` | New files; old files removed by `sync --delete` |
112	| `/index.html` | `no-cache` | Invalidated |
113	
114	## 7. Usage measurement
115	
116	CloudFront access logs go to the log bucket and are deleted after 90 days (Q6). This gives visit counts server-side, with no client code and no runtime network call. The logs contain visitor IP addresses. Legacy CloudFront standard logging requires ACLs enabled on the log bucket; newer log delivery options avoid this. Choose one during the build.
117	
118	## 8. Decision log (Theme 6)
119	
120	| ID | Question | Options considered | Decision |
121	|---|---|---|---|
122	| — | Conventions | OAC, us-east-1 certificate, hashed-asset caching, CSP, bootstrap stack | Accepted |
123	| Q1 | What happens on a PR into `prod`? | Build and test only; preview deploys; deploy to production | Build and test only; deploy on push to `prod` |
124	| Q2 | Who runs Terraform? | Local apply with CI sync only; CI plan and apply; CI plan only | CI runs plan on PRs and apply on push to `prod` (see open item 1) |
125	| Q3 | Where is the yoschwab.com DNS zone? | Route 53 same account; other provider with delegation; Route 53 other account | Route 53, same account |
126	| Q4 | Deploy role trust scope? | `prod` branch; `production` environment; environment plus approval | `prod` branch |
127	| Q5 | Where do account-specific values live? | Hard-coded; GitHub variables; GitHub secrets | GitHub secrets |
128	| Q6 | Measure usage? | None; CloudFront access logs; client analytics | CloudFront access logs to S3, 90-day lifecycle |
129	
130	## 9. Open items
131	
132	1. **Conflict between Q2 and Q4.** Q2 calls for `terraform plan` on pull requests, but Q4 scopes the deploy role's trust to the `prod` branch. PR runs present a `pull_request` subject, so they cannot assume the role and cannot plan. Options:
133	   - **(a)** Drop PR plans. `deploy.yml` runs plan immediately before apply on push to `prod`. Simplest.
134	   - **(b)** Add a second, read-only plan role trusted for `repo:<owner>/<repo>:pull_request`. Same-repo PRs get a plan; fork PRs get no OIDC token anyway.
135	   - **(c)** Widen the deploy role's trust. Not recommended, because it gives PR runs write access.
136	2. The subdomain name (to be provided).
137	3. Log delivery mechanism (§7).
138	4. Where the bootstrap stack's local state is kept safely off the repo.

Assistant2026-09-23T04:36:56.946Z

I've reviewed all six docs, and this host turns out to be the benchmark box: both A100-PCIE-40GB cards at 250 W, plus Node 22, pnpm, Terraform 1.10.5, gh, aws and uv. vLLM isn't installed yet.

Verdict

The plan is well scoped. The fixed-capacity framing, the staged progression, the decision logs and the calibration pipeline are all solid. Nothing I found sinks the project, so I'll go ahead with 00-build.md. I did find some real problems: one lesson is physically wrong, the scale estimate looks about 10× low, and two things are ambiguous in ways only you can settle.

1. Simulator reasoning

Wrong or at risk

  1. Tab 1's lesson ("TPOT does not scale with prompt length") breaks on long prompts, which is what the tab is about. Your own roofline (02 §6) has each decode step read 128 KiB × context of KV. At ~120k tokens that's ~15.7 GB, about the size of the weights, so batch-1 TPOT roughly doubles. It's only ~+6% at 8k and ~+26% at 32k. A faithful simulator will contradict the sidebar text.
  2. The "~1M events" estimate for a Server B week looks about 10× low. Rough numbers:
  3. A replica limited by KV decodes about 1k tokens/s, which is roughly 3 requests/s.
  4. Reaching the knee therefore takes several hundred analysts per replica.
  5. A Server B week at knee-level load is then ~1.5–2M requests and ~10–15M events.
  6. Near the knee, event-jumping saves about 10×, not 100×.

In JS that plausibly means 10–30 s of blocking main-thread work at every tab load and every fork. Per-request records stored as JS objects would take about 0.5 GB. 3. Fork-at-playhead needs checkpoints. After a full-week run, only the final state exists, so there's nothing to snapshot "at time t" without periodic checkpoints. 4. Separate PRNG streams don't make policy comparisons paired. A policy change reorders completions, so length and think-time draws land on different requests. The fix is draws keyed by (session, turn, attempt). 5. E2E latency hides the knee. Because output lengths are lognormal, E2E p99 is already 3–5× the mean at idle. TTFT is the clean signal for queueing.

Underspecified, and this is where the product lives

  1. Trigger vs. scheduled incident conflict. Q13 puts the incident on a fixed day, §10 describes a one-click trigger, and the tracked analyst must "span the incident". On tabs 1–3 the "trigger" is really a parameter change.
  2. A Friday incident's rollup arrives Saturday 12:00, which is outside a Mon–Fri horizon.
  3. Client behaviour is undefined. Is the timeout measured to first byte or to the full response? After retries run out, is the session abandoned or is the turn skipped? The retry-storm metrics depend on these answers.
  4. Tab 4's lesson sits in a narrow parameter window. With hundreds of sessions sharing a ~140k-token pool, LRU churn can evict histories during think time even under perfect affinity. Then tab 4 shows nothing.
  5. Stage (c) doesn't fit into tabs 4–6. It has 11 concepts and steps c1–c5 but only three tabs. Step c4 (hotspots, fleet p99) and concepts 6–8 have no tab.

2. Value proposition and scope

  • The value proposition is clear and differentiated for the stated audience. The High-side toggle is what no tool in your prior-art list has.
  • Missing: why you're building it, and what "done" means. A presenter-led demo and a self-serve link call for different priorities.
  • The scope hangs together, but v1 is large. I'd ship tabs 1–3 on one GPU as a deployable slice before stage (c). Tabs 5–6 carry most of the schedule risk.

3. Stack and hosting

The choices are sound: S3 + CloudFront + OAC, branch-scoped OIDC, actions pinned to full commit SHAs, and a CSP backing up the lint rules. Hosting should cost a few dollars a month at most. GitHub Pages can't set CSP headers, so AWS is justified. Problems:

  • CI only runs on PRs into prod. All the agent work on main gets no CI.
  • d3 transitions on React-owned SVG will fight React. Animate the data instead and let React render.
  • Workers: Vite's inline workers (?worker&inline) load from blob: URLs, which worker-src 'self' blocks.
  • Closing three open items: option (a) for the Terraform plan conflict, CloudFront standard logging v2, and migrating the bootstrap state into the state bucket.
  • Unit-only testing is thin for a discrete-event simulator whose risks come from interactions between parts.

4. UI/UX

  • Biggest issue: timescale. A week is 432,000 s, so even 1000× takes 7 minutes. Dots are only legible at 1–10×, where you'd wait hours between events. Nothing says where a tab opens; if it's Monday 00:00, the canvas is empty. Each tab needs a defined starting point, a "jump to incident" control, and off-shift hours skipped automatically.
  • The layout needs about 850 px of height, which breaks at 1280×720, a common projector and screen-share size.
  • Smaller points:
  • Add an explicit Reset.
  • The rollup table reaches 120 cells in a 30%-width sidebar.
  • On tab 1, per-bucket p99 over one analyst is meaningless.
  • Add keyboard control and reduced-motion support.

5. Ready to hand off?

  • Ready: the architecture, engine semantics, calibration and infra.
  • Not ready: the scenarios. Default parameters, incident times, starting points, acceptance criteria and sidebar text are all deferred to "during the build", yet they are the product. The build plan will make them explicit work packages with measurable acceptance criteria.

Some questions before I write the plan:

Each tab's work week contains its **lesson moment** at a scheduled day and time (K1). On tabs 5 and 6 that moment is an incident (a replica crash). On tabs 1–3 it is a workload change: a long prompt, a rate increase, or long conversations. On tab 4 it is a busy stretch under round-robin. Because the moment is in the baseline week, the timeline can mark it, the High-side rollup can show it, and the tracked analyst can be chosen to span it. The tab's one-click trigger forks the run at the playhead and re-applies the same change there, so the visitor can experiment. Lesson moments fall Monday to Thursday so the next day's rollup arrives inside the week (K2).

−
Status: scoping complete (September 2026).
Related docs: 01-product-and-rationale (lessons and scope), 03-benchmarks (calibration inputs), 04-stack (runtime).

## 1. Principles

- A pure TypeScript engine with no DOM access. Every random draw comes from a seeded stream.
- Discrete-event simulation with event-jumping: time jumps from one event to the next instead of ticking every engine step.
+
Status: scoping complete; kickoff review applied (September 2026).
Related docs: 00-build (build plan), 01-product-and-rationale (lessons and scope), 03-benchmarks (calibration inputs), 04-stack (runtime).

## 1. Principles

- A pure TypeScript engine with no DOM access. It runs in a Web Worker, advances incrementally, and checkpoints its state (04 §3, K11).
- Every random draw is keyed: a pure function of the seed, the random source, and the entity it belongs to (§12, K6).
- Discrete-event simulation with event-jumping: time jumps from one event to the next instead of ticking every engine step.

−
Expected scale: a Server B work week is on the order of 1M events, against tens of millions of engine steps.

Expected scale (revised, K7). A replica limited by KV serves roughly 3 requests/s at ~300-token outputs, so reaching the knee takes several hundred analysts per replica. A Server B work week at knee-level load is then about 1.5–2M requests and 10–15M events, against tens of millions of engine steps. Near the knee a new arrival lands every ~10 decode steps, so event-jumping saves about one order of magnitude, not two. These are estimates; the scale spike in 00-build (S1) measures the real figures.

−
- **Session turns:** turn N+1 is scheduled at turn N's arrival plus a think time. If turn N is still running, turn N+1 waits client-side until turn N completes. Load stays open-loop across the population, and each session stays coherent.
- **Session history:** turn N+1's prompt = system prompt + all prior user messages and outputs + the new message. Shared system prompt length is a parameter.
- **Distributions:** parametric (Theme 2 Q5):

+
- **Session turns:** turn N+1 is scheduled at turn N's arrival plus a think time. If turn N is still running, turn N+1 waits client-side until turn N completes. Load stays open-loop across the population, and each session stays coherent. Under overload this means turn N+1 follows turn N's completion with no think time. That is deliberate, because it keeps load open-loop.
- **Session history:** turn N+1's prompt = system prompt + all prior user messages and outputs + the new message. Shared system prompt length is a parameter.
- **Session scripts (K6):** when a session is created, its whole script is sampled from keyed draws: turn count, each turn's message and output lengths, and each think time. Policy changes therefore never reassign lengths between requests.
- **Distributions:** parametric (Theme 2 Q5):
  - new-message length and output length: lognormal
  - turns per session: geometric
  - think time: parametric distribution, family chosen during build
- **Client behavior (K8):**
  - The timeout runs to the first token. A streaming client gives up when nothing has arrived; once tokens flow, there is no timeout.
  - Retry policies are immediate, fixed, exponential, and full jitter. Jitter is keyed by (request, attempt). Max retries is a parameter.
  - When a turn exhausts its retries, it fails and the analyst abandons the session, sending no further turns. Abandoned sessions are counted.
  - Failed, aborted, and rejected turns never enter session history.

Assistant2026-09-23T04:47:47.712Z
✏️ Edit 02-simulator.md
/home/user/yosyp-work/inference-simulator/docs/02-simulator.md
−
- **Seeded streams:** one PRNG stream per random source (arrivals, lengths, think times, failures, tie-breaks). Changing a policy never shifts arrival times, so policy comparisons are paired.
+
- **Keyed draws (K6):** every draw comes from a counter-based PRNG and is a pure function of (seed, source, entity key). Examples: (session) for session start and script, (session, turn) for lengths and think time, (request, attempt) for retry jitter, (replica, incident) for failures. Separate sequential streams per source are not enough. A policy change reorders completions, so sequential draws would land on different requests and break pairing. With keyed draws, changing a policy never shifts arrival times or reassigns lengths, so policy comparisons are paired.

| K6 | Do per-source PRNG streams keep policy comparisons paired? | Sequential stream per source; keyed counter-based draws | Keyed draws with per-session scripts sampled at creation (§8, §12) |
| K7 | Scale and per-request storage | Keep the ~1M-event estimate; revise and budget | Revised to ~10–15M events for a Server B week; columnar storage; a memory budget set in 00-build; S1 decides between full records and re-simulating detail on demand |
| K8 | Client semantics | Timeout to first token vs. total; on retry exhaustion skip the turn vs. abandon the session | Timeout to first token; the session is abandoned after retries are exhausted; failed turns never enter history |
| K9 | Form of the admission limit (open item 2) | Queue at the router; per-replica in-flight cap; fleet outstanding cap | Fleet outstanding cap = per-replica limit × Ready replicas, tracked exactly by the router |

## 14. Open items

1. Default values for all load-generator distributions and client timeout and retry parameters.
2. Default admission limit per replica (form decided in K9).
3. Default router signal refresh delay and crash detection delay.
4. Lesson-moment day and time per tab: Monday to Thursday (K2); exact values are set in scenario tuning.
5. Tab 4 parameter window. Under affinity, a session's history must usually survive its think time in the KV pool, or the routing lesson disappears. With hundreds of sessions per replica, LRU churn can evict histories even under perfect affinity. The tab 4 lesson assertions in 00-build check this.

## 10. Open items

1. Exact sweep points for R1–R3, and the hold duration per rate step.
2. Whether to model weight transfer to a replacement host as a network-speed parameter (currently excluded).
3. Fit quality of a single η_c. Attention is about two-thirds of prefill FLOPs at 120k tokens and about a third at 32k, and FlashAttention efficiency differs from GEMM efficiency. The derivation reports residuals across R1's range. If they exceed about 15% at either end, split η_c into GEMM and attention efficiencies (calibration schema v2).
4. Manifests are published. Scrub hostnames, usernames, and tokens before committing raw data.
